How the Work Unfolds
There is a natural order to this work.
Not because it was designed that way—but because every attempt to shortcut it fails in the same place.
Businesses want solutions.
What they actually need is clarity.
And clarity, when it’s built on evidence instead of assumption, can only be reached one way:
By reconstructing what already worked before deciding what to do next.
Everything Legaisee does follows that sequence.
Nothing is added prematurely.
Nothing is installed without context.
And nothing moves forward until what came before it is fully understood.

Stage One — The Audit
Every engagement begins here.
Not with strategy. Not with recommendations.
With reconstruction.
Your business is treated as a site—one that has been active for years, shaped by decisions, influenced by people, and layered with outcomes that still exist even if no one is actively referencing them anymore.
We begin by locating those layers.
Public records are only the surface. Anyone can access them, and most firms stop there.
We don’t.
Internal materials are examined—old campaigns, archived files, forgotten documents, anything that still carries a trace of how the business once operated.
When access allows, conversations are conducted with people who were there when key decisions were made. Not to rely on memory, but to compare it against what the documentation reveals.
Patterns are identified, not from a single source, but from convergence.
When multiple signals point to the same structure, it becomes something that can be trusted.
From that, a timeline emerges.
There is always a period where performance peaked—not just in revenue, but in clarity. Messaging aligned. Positioning held. Customers responded without resistance.
That period is isolated.
Then something interrupts it.
A shift. A replacement. A slow deviation that, over time, becomes a different system entirely.
That point is identified as precisely as the evidence allows.
Not to assign fault.
To understand cause.
What you receive at the end of this process is not a summary.
It is a documented reconstruction.
A record of what worked, when it worked, why it worked, and where it was lost.
Not interpreted through current trends.
Preserved in its original structure, then translated into what it would require to operate under current conditions.
This is the foundation.
Without it, everything else is guesswork.

Stage Two — The Build
Once the original advantage is understood, the question changes.
It is no longer, “What should we do?”
It becomes, “How do we make this operate again—at scale, under modern conditions, without degrading over time?”
That is where most efforts fail.
Because recognizing what worked is not the same as being able to execute it consistently.
What existed before was often dependent on individuals.
Specific people who knew how to communicate a certain way, structure offers a certain way, make decisions with a certain level of instinct.
When those people left, the system went with them.
Stage Two removes that dependency.
The patterns recovered in the audit are translated into systems that can be operated deliberately.
Your communication style is captured—not superficially, but structurally. Vocabulary, rhythm, tone, decision framing, the way authority is expressed without forcing it.
From that, production systems are built.
Content is no longer created from scratch each time. It is generated, refined, and deployed through a repeatable process that maintains consistency without requiring constant oversight.
Publishing becomes structured.
Not reactive. Not sporadic.
Deliberate.
Timed. Sequenced. Aligned with how your business actually moves customers toward decisions.
Performance is no longer something you “check.”
It becomes part of the system itself.
Feedback loops are installed so results inform adjustments continuously—without requiring full resets or reinvention.
And everything that is produced is stored, organized, and retrievable.
Not as a collection of files.
As a working archive.
So nothing that performs well is ever lost again.
By the time this stage is complete, your business is no longer dependent on memory, guesswork, or inconsistent execution.
It has a system.
One that reflects what made it effective in the first place—now operating with more control than it ever had before.

Stage Three — The Partnership
Systems, once built, don’t maintain themselves.
They drift.
Not immediately. Not obviously.
But over time, small deviations begin to appear.
Standards loosen. Decisions get made under pressure. Shortcuts re-enter the process.
Left alone, even strong systems degrade.
Stage Three exists to prevent that.
This is not management in the traditional sense.
It is oversight with context.
Performance is reviewed regularly—not just in terms of metrics, but in alignment with the original structure that made the system work.
When something shifts, it is corrected early.
Before it compounds.
New information is introduced as it becomes available.
Competitors are observed—not to copy, but to understand how the market is responding and where new gaps are forming.
Additional opportunities are identified through continued excavation.
Because the initial audit rarely uncovers everything.
It reveals enough to rebuild.
But there are always deeper layers.
This stage ensures the system doesn’t just operate.
It evolves without losing its foundation.

Stage Four — Authority
There is a point where the business itself is no longer the only asset.
The individual behind it becomes one.
Not as a personality.
As a signal.
Authority, when established correctly, reduces friction across everything else.
Content lands faster. Trust forms quicker. Decisions require less persuasion.
But most attempts at building authority feel forced.
Because they are built on performance instead of structure.
Stage Four corrects that.
The same patterns recovered from your business are applied to how you communicate directly.
On camera. In writing. In moments where attention is earned, not assumed.
Presence is not manufactured.
It is aligned.
So when you speak, it carries the same clarity your business once did at its strongest point.
Not louder.
Sharper.
And once that alignment is in place, authority is no longer something you try to build.
It becomes something that is recognized.

Access
Not every business qualifies for this work.
Not because of industry.
Because of depth.
If there is no history, there is nothing to reconstruct.
If there has been no real investment in the past, there is no pattern to recover.
And if access to the people and materials that shaped the business is restricted, the work cannot be done properly.
For those that do qualify, the process is direct.
It begins with a brief entry point—a limited excavation designed to determine whether something worth recovering exists.
From there, the full audit is scheduled.
Everything else follows in sequence.
Nothing is rushed.
Nothing is skipped.
Because when this is done correctly, it does not need to be done again.

Closing
Most businesses believe their next breakthrough will come from something new.
A new platform. A new strategy. A new direction.
Sometimes it does.
Most of the time, it doesn’t.
Because the strongest advantage they’ve ever had is already behind them—
Unrecognized, undocumented, and unused.
Until it’s recovered.
Legaisee exists to do exactly that.
And once it’s found—
To make sure it works again.

LEGAISEE — METHODOLOGY (FINAL COPY)

The Discipline Behind the Work
Most marketing operates on interpretation.
Trends are observed. Data is sampled. Conclusions are drawn quickly, often under pressure to produce something actionable.
It works—until it doesn’t.
Because interpretation, by nature, shifts with context.
What Legaisee applies instead is closer to documentation.
Not in the sense of recording what is happening now.
But in reconstructing what has already happened—with enough precision that it can be relied on again.
This is the difference between forming an opinion and establishing a record.
One adapts.
The other holds.

Where Most Analyses Fail
When businesses attempt to understand their own history, they tend to rely on two sources.
Memory and surface-level data.
Memory is selective. It compresses time, removes context, and reshapes events around outcomes that are easier to explain in hindsight.
Surface data is incomplete. It shows what was visible, not what drove the result.
Taken together, they create a version of the past that feels accurate—
But rarely is.
This is where most strategic decisions begin.
And why so many of them drift.
Because they are built on narratives that were never verified.

Reconstruction, Not Recall
The work begins by removing the assumption that anyone fully remembers what happened.
Including the people who were there.
Instead of asking for explanations, we look for traces.
A campaign that appears across multiple formats. Language that repeats with minor variation. Offers that resurface over time with consistent structure.
These are not accidents.
They are signals.
When the same pattern appears in different places, under different conditions, it becomes something that can be trusted.
Not because someone said it worked.
Because the evidence shows that it did.
From there, reconstruction begins.
Not as a story.
As a system.

On Certainty
Not all information carries equal weight.
Some findings are supported directly—documents, records, materials that can be verified without interpretation.
Others are inferred—supported by multiple signals, but not preserved in full.
Some remain uncertain.
Most firms blur these distinctions.
Legaisee does not.
Every conclusion is treated according to the strength of the evidence behind it.
When something is clear, it is stated directly.
When something is probable, it is framed accordingly.
When something cannot be confirmed, it is left as such.
This is not hesitation.
It is control.
Because decisions built on overstated certainty tend to fail the moment reality contradicts them.
Decisions built on clearly defined confidence levels hold.

When Sources Conflict
They always do.
A previous owner remembers events one way. An employee recalls them differently. A document suggests something else entirely.
Most analyses resolve this by choosing the version that fits the desired conclusion.
That approach produces clean narratives.
And unreliable strategies.
Legaisee handles conflict differently.
All versions are preserved.
Each is evaluated based on proximity, detail, and potential bias.
Then a synthesis is constructed—not by forcing agreement, but by identifying what can coexist logically across sources.
What remains is not perfect.
But it is far closer to reality than any single account on its own.
And more importantly, it is transparent.

The Structure Beneath Execution
What ultimately matters is not the individual tactic.
Not the specific advertisement, or campaign, or platform.
Those change.
What matters is the structure that allowed those tactics to work.
The way a message was framed. The position it occupied in the market. The sequence through which a customer moved from awareness to decision without resistance.
These are rarely documented explicitly.
But they leave consistent traces.
Once identified, they can be isolated from their original format and reassembled under current conditions.
Not copied.
Reconstructed.
So they function the same way, even if they appear different on the surface.

Voice as System
Most attempts to replicate communication focus on style.
Choice of words. Tone. Surface-level adjustments that make something feel similar without actually being consistent.
That approach breaks quickly.
Because real communication patterns are structural.
They exist in how ideas are introduced, how tension is built, how authority is expressed without forcing it.
Legaisee captures this at the level where it can be repeated.
Not by imitation.
By mapping the underlying decisions that shape how something is said.
From there, systems can produce output that aligns naturally—without requiring constant correction.
The result is not content that sounds similar.
It is content that behaves the same way.

Preservation
Most businesses lose their advantage the same way they lost it the first time.
Gradually.
Not because the system failed.
Because it wasn’t preserved.
Files scatter. Processes change. New people reinterpret old decisions without context.
Over time, what was once deliberate becomes inconsistent again.
Legaisee treats preservation as part of the methodology, not an afterthought.
Everything that is built is documented in a way that can be followed.
Everything that performs is stored in a way that can be retrieved.
So the system does not depend on any single person remembering how it works.
It exists independently.
And continues to function even as the business evolves.

The Principle That Holds Everything Together
There is a reason this approach works across industries.
Because it is not dependent on the market.
It is dependent on evidence.
Every business that has operated successfully over time has left behind a record of how it did so.
Incomplete, often disorganized—but present.
Most never examine it closely enough to extract anything useful.
Those that do gain something different from an idea.
They gain a reference point.
Something real enough to build from.
Stable enough to return to.
And precise enough to operate without guessing.

Closing
The difference between speculation and certainty is not confidence.
It is proof.
Most strategy is built on the former.
Legaisee is built on the latter.
Not to eliminate change.
But to ensure that whatever changes next—
Is anchored to something that has already been proven to work.

LEGAISEE — CASE STUDIES (FINAL COPY)

What This Looks Like in Practice
Every business believes its situation is unique.
Different industry. Different timeline. Different set of problems.
And on the surface, that’s true.
But once the excavation begins, the pattern underneath is almost always familiar.
There was a period where everything worked.
Then something shifted.
And no one ever fully documented why.
What follows are not success stories in the traditional sense.
They are reconstructions—moments where something lost was found again, understood clearly, and returned to operation.

Case One — The Membership That Disappeared
The company had been operating for over two decades.
Stable. Known locally. Consistent enough that no one questioned the model—until growth stopped.
Not declined.
Stopped.
Revenue flattened despite a market that was still expanding. Competitors began gaining ground, not by doing anything extraordinary, but by appearing more relevant.
When asked what had changed, the answer was immediate.
“Nothing.”
That was the problem.
Because something always changes.
The excavation started with surface materials—recent campaigns, current messaging, pricing structure.
Everything looked reasonable.
Nothing explained the plateau.
The shift appeared further back.
A set of campaigns from years earlier—structured differently, positioned differently, repeated with minor variation across multiple seasons.
They weren’t promotional.
They were membership-based.
A recurring offer that reframed the relationship entirely. Customers weren’t buying services when something broke.
They were maintaining access to a system.
The materials were still there.
Old mailers. Pricing sheets. Fragments of customer responses preserved in ways no one had revisited.
When compared against revenue timelines, the pattern was clear.
During the years the membership was active, a disproportionate percentage of revenue came from it.
When it was removed, nothing replaced it.
Not intentionally.
It was simply abandoned.
A new decision-maker had shifted toward discount-based acquisition, assuming it would scale faster.
It did.
Briefly.
Then it eroded the very positioning that had made the membership effective in the first place.
The recognition, when it landed, was immediate.
Not dramatic.
Quiet.
The kind that doesn’t require explanation once it’s seen.
The restoration didn’t replicate the original.
It rebuilt the structure.
The offer returned in a modern form. Delivery adjusted. Systems installed to support it consistently.
Within months, recurring revenue reappeared—not as an experiment, but as something the business had already proven it could sustain.
Nothing new was invented.
Something old was understood correctly.

Case Two — The Reputation No One Was Using
The firm had history.
Three generations.
A name that, depending on who you asked, still carried weight.
But only with certain people.
Older clients would reference it. Mention things the current leadership had never seen, never documented, never thought to use.
New clients saw something else entirely.
A firm competing on availability and price.
The excavation didn’t begin with campaigns.
It began with the basement.
Boxes that had not been opened in years.
Inside them, a record.
Not polished. Not curated.
But consistent.
A publication—distributed regularly, maintained over time, written in a tone that didn’t attempt to sell.
It explained.
It guided.
It positioned the firm as something other than a service provider.
An authority.
The response data, where it still existed, supported it.
High engagement. Repeat readership. A level of trust that reduced resistance before a conversation ever started.
It had been discontinued with the assumption that something newer would replace it.
It never did.
The market didn’t forget.
The firm did.
What followed was not a revival of the publication as it was.
It was a reconstruction of the role it played.
A system that allowed the same type of communication to exist again—without relying on the same people, without requiring the same effort to maintain.
The voice was preserved.
The structure was rebuilt.
And over time, something subtle began to shift.
New clients started referencing content before the first conversation.
Not asking if the firm could help.
Assuming it.
The reputation had never disappeared.
It had simply stopped being expressed.

Case Three — The Authority That Was Replaced by Price
The company had reduced itself without realizing it.
Not in size.
In position.
Competing on price had become normal. Expected. Necessary, according to the internal logic of the team.
Margins reflected it.
Thin. Controlled only through volume.
When asked why the business operated that way, the answer was practical.
“That’s what the market responds to.”
The excavation suggested otherwise.
Earlier materials told a different story.
Technical depth. Detailed explanations. A level of expertise that had once been central to how the company communicated.
It wasn’t hidden.
It was abandoned.
Not because it stopped working.
Because it required more effort to maintain.
The transition to price-based competition hadn’t been strategic.
It had been convenient.
And over time, it became identity.
The recovery focused on structure, not content.
Reintroducing complexity where it mattered. Reframing communication around knowledge instead of cost.
The business didn’t become more expensive overnight.
It became more precise.
And with that precision, something shifted.
Conversations changed.
Instead of negotiating price, prospects began asking questions.
Not “how much.”
But “how does this work?”
That change, small on the surface, altered everything behind it.
Margins followed.
Not because pricing increased arbitrarily.
Because the business returned to a position where it no longer had to justify itself through cost.

Case Four — The Catalog That Outperformed the Website
The transition had been treated as progress.
Print to digital.
Catalog to website.
Physical to scalable.
The logic was sound.
The results were not.
Online traffic increased.
Engagement metrics appeared healthy.
Revenue did not follow.
Not at the level it once had.
The assumption was execution.
Something about the digital strategy needed improvement.
The excavation went further back.
Decades of materials—catalogs that had evolved over time.
Not just as product listings.
As something else entirely.
They told stories.
Not explicitly.
But consistently enough that customers didn’t just browse.
They imagined.
When matched against purchase behavior, the difference became obvious.
Catalog-driven customers spent more.
Returned more.
Stayed longer.
The catalog had not been a sales tool.
It had been an environment.
The website, for all its efficiency, had removed that.
What followed was not a reversal.
It was a reconstruction.
Reintroducing depth where speed had replaced it. Creating a format that didn’t compete with digital—but restored what digital had removed.
When it returned, it didn’t feel outdated.
It felt intentional.
Response followed.
Not because print was superior.
Because the structure behind it had been restored.

Closing
None of these outcomes came from guessing.
Or from chasing what was working somewhere else.
They came from identifying something that had already worked—understanding it without distortion—and putting it back into operation with control.
Every business that has operated long enough has a version of this.
Something that carried more weight than what replaced it.
Something that, once seen clearly, changes how every decision after it is made.
The question is not whether it exists.
It’s whether anyone takes the time to find it.

THE SHALLOW DIG
Hero Section
Headline:
 There’s Something Buried in Your Business.
 We Can Surface It in 15 Minutes.

 Not a report. Not a pitch.
 A glimpse of what you used to do so well… it worked without trying.
We run a rapid excavation across your digital and historical footprint—
 and send you one undeniable finding worth paying attention to.
No call required.
 No pressure afterward.
 Just evidence.
Primary CTA:
 Get Your 15-Minute Snapshot

The Moment of Recognition
There’s a moment most business owners don’t talk about.
It doesn’t happen during a campaign.
 It doesn’t show up on a dashboard.
It hits when you look at your business and quietly think:
“We used to be better than this.”
Not bigger. Not busier.
 Better.
More respected.
 More certain.
 More… obvious.
Customers chose you faster.
 Your message landed harder.
 Your position in the market didn’t need to be explained.
And then—without a clean break—it drifted.
A new hire changed direction.
 A platform shifted.
 A strategy got replaced before it was understood.
Nothing failed loudly enough to investigate.
 So everything slowly diluted.
The Shallow Dig exists for that exact moment.

What We Actually Do (Without Saying Everything)
You give us almost nothing.
A business name.
 A website.
 An industry.
From there, we begin pulling threads.
Old versions of your site.
 Fragments of messaging you no longer use.
 Patterns in how you used to present yourself.
 Signals your competitors reacted to—whether you noticed or not.
We’re not looking for activity.
We’re looking for inflection points.
Moments where something worked disproportionately well…
 and then quietly disappeared.
Within minutes, those patterns start to reassemble.
Not fully.
 Just enough to recognize.

What You Receive
What arrives in your inbox isn’t a “lead magnet.”
It’s a single-page document that feels… specific.
Not broad insights.
 Not generic advice.
Something like:
A moment in time where your business was clearly positioned—and why
 A piece of messaging or structure you abandoned that still has leverage
 A shift that likely caused the decline you’ve been feeling but couldn’t name
 A question that—once seen—is hard to ignore
You won’t get ten ideas.
You’ll get one that matters.

Why This Is Free
Because anything less wouldn’t be believed.
If we asked you to commit before showing you anything,
 you’d assume this was another strategy conversation dressed up as insight.
So we removed the barrier.
We run the system.
 We send the result.
 You decide if it’s worth exploring further.
Most people don’t need convincing after they see it.
They need context.

What This Is Not
This is not a full excavation.
 It won’t map your entire history.
 It won’t rebuild your systems.
It’s a surface-level dig—just deep enough to expose something real.
And once you see it,
 you’ll understand exactly why it’s there.

Who This Works For
This only works if there’s something to find.
Businesses with time behind them.
 Decisions layered over years.
 Moments of clarity that were never documented properly.
If everything you’ve done is recent,
 there’s nothing buried yet.
But if your business has lived through phases—
 growth, change, reinvention, compromise—
this tends to land harder than expected.

The Quiet Shift
Most people expect one of two outcomes.
Either nothing interesting shows up…
 or they get something vaguely useful.
What actually happens is different.
They recognize something immediately.
Not intellectually.
 Viscerally.
And once that happens, the conversation changes.
Not “what should we do?”
 But “why did we ever stop?”

Final Section
You don’t need a call to start.
You don’t need to prepare anything.
You don’t need to believe in the process.
You just need to be curious enough to check.

No movement (no sliding, no bouncing)

 opacity: 0;
 transition: opacity 0.2s ease;
}

JAVASCRIPT (CONTROLLED + LIGHTWEIGHT)
<script>
const insightBox = document.getElementById('timelineInsight');

document.querySelectorAll('[data-insight]').forEach(el => {
 el.addEventListener('mouseenter', () => {
   insightBox.textContent = el.getAttribute('data-insight');
   insightBox.style.opacity = 1;
 });

 el.addEventListener('mouseleave', () => {
   insightBox.style.opacity = 0;
 });

 // Mobile tap support
 el.addEventListener('click', () => {
   insightBox.textContent = el.getAttribute('data-insight');
   insightBox.style.opacity = 1;
 });
});
</script>

WHY THIS WORKS (KEEP THIS INTACT)
This interaction does something subtle but powerful:
It invites exploration without demanding it
It rewards curiosity instead of interrupting flow
It turns a static model into a self-recognition mechanism
The user isn’t being told what happened.
They’re discovering it—one phase at a time.

Good—this is where your system stops being a “website feature” and becomes proprietary infrastructure.
You’re essentially asking:
 How do we bind user-submitted data → AI snapshot output → UI timeline → micro-interactions in a way that feels instantaneous and intelligent?
Here’s the clean, production-grade architecture and copy logic—no fluff.

SYSTEM OVERVIEW (WHAT YOU’RE BUILDING)
You are creating a 3-layer synchronization loop:
Input Layer → user submits business data
Intelligence Layer → AI generates structured findings
Experience Layer → timeline renders + updates dynamically
The timeline is NOT visual decoration.
 It is a data visualization endpoint.

1. DATA STRUCTURE (THIS CONTROLS EVERYTHING)
Your Snapshot output must return structured fields—not just text.
Required JSON output from your AI system:
{
 "goldenEra": {
   "start": "2008",
   "end": "2012",
   "label": "Membership-driven growth era",
   "insight": "Recurring revenue and retention dominated performance"
 },
 "shift": {
   "year": "2013",
   "label": "Strategic pivot to discount positioning",
   "insight": "Brand authority diluted in favor of price competition"
 },
 "fragmentation": {
   "start": "2014",
   "end": "2022",
   "label": "Loss of identity",
   "insight": "Inconsistent messaging and declining differentiation"
 },
 "current": {
   "year": "2026",
   "label": "Undervalued legacy position",
   "insight": "Strong foundation remains but is not leveraged"
 }
}
This is non-negotiable.
If your AI returns paragraphs instead of structured data →
 your entire interactive system breaks.

2. FRONT-END TIMELINE ENGINE
Now the timeline becomes a data-mapped component, not static HTML.
Base states (fixed structure):
[Growth] —— [Peak] —— [Shift] —— [Fragmentation] —— [Now]
Dynamic overlays (from AI):
Golden Era → spans Growth → Peak
Marker → positioned proportionally between years
Labels → injected dynamically
Hover copy → pulled from JSON

3. POSITIONING LOGIC (CRITICAL DETAIL)
You must convert dates into relative positions.
Example:
const startYear = 2000;
const currentYear = 2026;

function getPosition(year) {
 return ((year - startYear) / (currentYear - startYear)) * 100;
}
Then:
goldenStart = getPosition(2008);
goldenEnd = getPosition(2012);
shiftPoint = getPosition(2013);
This gives you:
Golden Era bar width
Marker placement
Timeline accuracy per business

4. MICRO-INTERACTION SYSTEM (HOVER INTELLIGENCE)
Each node becomes a data trigger.
Hover behavior:
Growth
“Early expansion phase detected. Signals of initial traction and positioning clarity.”
Peak (Golden Era)
“Peak performance period: 2008–2012
 Primary driver: Membership retention system
 This is your highest-leverage restoration point.”
Shift
“2013: Strategic deviation detected
 Movement away from authority positioning toward price competition.”
Fragmentation
“Extended inconsistency period
 Messaging dilution and competitive erosion observed.”
Now
“Current state: Recoverable advantage
 Core assets remain intact but underutilized.”

5. LIVE SYNC FLOW (AFTER FORM SUBMISSION)
This is the sequence:
Step 1 — User submits form
→ POST to backend
Step 2 — Backend runs:
AI queries
Data extraction
JSON structuring
Step 3 — Response returned:
{
 "snapshotText": "...",
 "timelineData": {...above structure...}
}
Step 4 — Frontend receives response
Immediately:
Replace placeholder timeline
Animate marker into position
Fade in labels
Enable hover states

6. VISUAL BEHAVIOR (IMPORTANT FOR PERCEPTION)
BEFORE submission:
Timeline is dim
No marker
No dates
Slight blur
AFTER submission:
Golden Era bar animates in (left → right)
Marker drops into place
Labels fade in
Hover becomes active
This creates the feeling:
“The system just discovered something about me.”
That’s the psychological win.

7. CONFIRMATION SCREEN INTEGRATION

Instead of:
 “Check your email”
You show:

“Excavation in Progress…”
“We’re reconstructing your business timeline now.”
(Timeline appears faint → begins populating)

Then transition to:

And the timeline locks into place.

8. WHY THIS MATTERS (STRATEGIC)
This does 3 things instantly:
1. Reduces cognitive load
People understand timelines faster than paragraphs.
2. Creates ownership
“This is MY business story.”
3. Builds authority
You didn’t “tell” them something
 You revealed it.

9. WHAT MOST PEOPLE MISS (YOU WON’T)

Show static graphics
Maybe fake personalization
You are:

Headline
Investment, Not Expense
Subheadline
Most companies spend money guessing what might work.
 We allocate capital toward what already has.

Opening Narrative
There’s a quiet difference between businesses that grow deliberately…
 and those that stay in motion but never seem to advance.
The difference isn’t effort.
 It isn’t intelligence.
 It isn’t even resources.
It’s where the investment goes.
Most marketing spend is forward-looking speculation—
 new platforms, new tactics, new agencies, new ideas.
Always chasing something unproven.
 Always paying to learn what doesn’t work.
We don’t operate there.
Legaisee is built on a different premise entirely:
Your business has already solved its hardest problems.
 It has already discovered what your market responds to.
 It has already produced leverage.
That value didn’t disappear.
It was buried.
What you’re investing in here…
 is not experimentation.
It’s recovery, reconstruction, and controlled amplification.

Transition
Before we discuss numbers, understand this:
We are not selling deliverables.
We are rebuilding a system that produces authority, consistency, and leverage—
 without requiring you to rediscover them the hard way.

Stage I — The Audit
Headline
The Excavation
Narrative
Every engagement begins the same way.
Not with assumptions.
 Not with strategy decks.
 Not with ideas.
With evidence.
We go into your business the way a forensic team approaches a site—
 quietly, methodically, without bias.
We trace back through campaigns, assets, messaging, decisions, and transitions.
We identify when things worked, why they worked,
 and—more importantly—when that changed.
Patterns begin to emerge.
Not guesses.
 Not opinions.
Patterns with timestamps.
By the time we’re finished, what you have in front of you
 is not a “report.”
It’s a reconstructed version of your business at its most effective.
Something you can see.
 Something you can verify.
 Something you can act on.
Investment
The Audit is typically a $2,000 to $7,500 engagement,
 depending on the depth of history and access required.
Most clients know within minutes of reviewing it
 that they’ve already recovered more value than they spent.

Stage II — The Build
Headline
Restoration Into System
Narrative
Insight without execution is where most companies stall.
They learn something valuable…
 and then nothing changes.
This is where we move from understanding → to implementation.
We take what was uncovered during excavation
 and rebuild it as a living system.
Your voice is reconstructed—not approximated.
 Your positioning is restored—not reinvented.
 Your messaging is aligned—not improvised.
Then we do something most firms never attempt:
We mechanize it.
Content creation becomes structured.
 Publishing becomes rhythmic.
 Feedback becomes measurable.
 Assets become reusable.
What used to require constant effort
 becomes a system your team can operate.
Not perfectly at first.
 But predictably.
And predictability is where leverage begins.
Investment
System builds typically range from $5,500 to $18,000,
 depending on complexity, team size, and integration depth.
This is where businesses stop “trying to stay consistent”
 and start operating with consistency built in.

Stage III — The Partnership
Headline
Ongoing Advantage
Narrative
Once the system is running, the question changes.
It’s no longer:
“What should we do?”
It becomes:
“How do we stay ahead?”
Markets shift.
 Competitors adapt.
 Opportunities open quietly, then disappear.
Left alone, even strong systems drift.
This stage exists to prevent that.
We remain involved—not as vendors,
 but as a strategic extension of your operation.
We monitor performance.
 We refine messaging.
 We identify shifts early.
 We correct course before decline becomes visible.
And when something breaks…
 we don’t schedule a meeting.
We fix it.
This is not constant reinvention.
It’s controlled evolution.
Investment
Partnership engagements typically begin around $2,750 per month
 and scale based on scope, responsiveness, and level of involvement.
Clients who remain here aren’t looking for help.
They’re protecting an advantage.

Stage IV — Authority Development
Headline
The Operator Becomes the Signal
Narrative
At a certain point, systems alone are not enough.
The market doesn’t just respond to structure.
 It responds to presence.
This stage is where the business and the individual align.
We work directly with you—on camera, in communication, in delivery—
 until your authority is not implied…
but recognized.
Not performative.
 Not artificial.
 Not rehearsed.
Clear.
 Natural.
 Controlled.
When done correctly, this changes everything.
Content stops feeling like output.
 It starts functioning as influence.
Investment
Authority development engagements typically range
 from $3,750 to $12,000 depending on depth and duration.
For the right operator, this becomes the highest-leverage shift available.

The Reality Section

Who This Is For

History.
If your company is new, there is nothing to excavate yet.
If your past efforts were minimal, there is little to reconstruct.
If you are looking for rapid spikes, shortcuts, or “viral” outcomes—

FAQ PAGE
Headline
Questions Worth Asking
Subheadline
Most of what matters isn’t obvious at first glance.
 These are the conversations that usually happen just before a decision is made.

How is Business Archaeology different from market research?
Market research looks outward.
It studies trends, competitors, and current demand—trying to determine what might work next.
There’s value in that. But it comes with a limitation.
It has no memory.
Business Archaeology looks inward first.
It asks a different question entirely:
“When did this business already work—and what, specifically, made that happen?”
Because success is not theoretical.
 It leaves evidence.
When we begin there, strategy stops being speculative.
It becomes reconstructive.

What if we don’t have archives, records, or old marketing materials?
Most businesses believe this at the beginning.
Very few are correct.
History doesn’t disappear.
 It fragments.
Some of it lives in public records.
 Some in overlooked files.
 Some in the memory of people who were there when things worked.
And some of it is visible only in patterns—shifts in positioning, changes in language, moments where momentum was gained or lost.
We don’t rely on a single source.
We triangulate.
And more often than not, what looked like “nothing”
 turns out to be enough.

How long does it take to see results?
Clarity comes quickly.
Within days, most clients see their business differently than they did before.
Execution takes longer—but it’s not indefinite.
Once systems are in place, early signals begin to shift within the first month.
Engagement changes.
 Consistency stabilizes.
 Direction becomes clear.
Meaningful business impact typically follows within one to three cycles of operation.
Not instantly.
But predictably.

Do we have to use AI?
No one is handing control to a machine.
The systems use AI for one reason:
Scale.
It allows your voice, your thinking, and your positioning
 to be expressed consistently without requiring your constant presence.
Nothing is published without your approval.
Nothing evolves without your input.
Over time, the system becomes more accurate—not more independent.
It reflects you.
It does not replace you.

What if we don’t like what you find?
That happens occasionally.
Not because the findings are wrong,
 but because they reveal something that had gone unnoticed.
A decision that shifted positioning.
 A strength that was abandoned.
 A moment where direction changed quietly but permanently.
We don’t present history as judgment.
We present it as context.
Because the past isn’t something you defend.
It’s something you use.

Can you work with our existing team?
In most cases, that’s exactly what happens.
Your team already understands your business.
 They already carry context, experience, and intuition.
What they often lack is structure.
Clarity around what to execute.
 Systems that make consistency possible.
 Feedback loops that guide improvement.
We don’t replace that.
We give it something to operate within.
The result is not disruption.
It’s alignment.

What types of businesses does this not work for?
This approach depends on something very specific:
A past that produced results.
If a business is too new, there is nothing to excavate yet.
If there has been no meaningful investment in marketing,
 there is little to reconstruct.
And if the objective is speed over substance—
 rapid spikes, short-term attention, or unpredictable outcomes—
this will feel unnecessarily deliberate.
That’s by design.

How do you handle confidential information?
Carefully, and without exception.
We operate with the same mindset as archivists working with private collections.
Access is controlled.
 Sources are protected.
 Sensitive material is handled according to your preference—retained, returned, or destroyed.
If discretion matters to you,
 you’ll find that it matters to us as well.

What happens after the audit?
That depends entirely on what we uncover.
Some clients see the findings and immediately recognize the path forward.
Others take time to consider.
There is no pressure to continue.
Because the audit stands on its own.
If we move forward, it’s because the value is clear—
 not because the process requires it.

Final Closing
Most decisions at this level don’t come down to information.
They come down to recognition.
You either see the opportunity…
or you don’t.
If you do, the next step is simple.
If you don’t, nothing here needs to convince you.

CTA
If you’re still reading, you already understand more than most.
Let’s take a look.

About Business Archaeology – Methodology
Business Archaeology is not marketing fluff. It is a disciplined investigative process, designed to uncover the historical truths of a business, its decisions, its trajectory, and its opportunities—so you can act with unmatched clarity and authority.
We do not guess. We research. We analyze. We verify. We synthesize.

Our Core Approach
We operate across eight major classes and 40+ specific channels, covering the spectrum from 1890s marketing campaigns to today’s digital footprint. Each channel is assessed on its historical impact, relevance, and authenticity. This is how we reconstruct your business’s narrative with surgical precision.

Eight Major Classes (High-Level Overview)
Advertising & Marketing – Print, radio, TV, digital, social campaigns.
Financial Records & Public Filings – Budgets, investor reports, tax filings.
Operations & Infrastructure – Physical assets, locations, supply chain decisions.
Digital Presence – Websites, social media, digital archives, SEO history.
Brand Voice & Communications – Copywriting, email campaigns, messaging evolution.
Personnel & Leadership – Founders, executives, staff decisions, key hires.

Each channel is assigned a tiered credibility score:
Tier 1 – Verified: Documented, confirmed, primary sources
Tier 2 – Probable: Highly reliable secondary sources
Tier 3 – Plausible: Contextual evidence, reasonable assumption
Tier 4 – Uncertain: Unverified claims, minor relevance
We do not allow significant claims without either 2 independent sources or 1 Tier 1 source. This is non-negotiable.

Triangulation Protocol
When sources conflict, we document, assess, and synthesize:
Record each source verbatim
Compare against other data points
Resolve discrepancies using weighted credibility
Capture unresolved conflicts transparently
This ensures your business’s history is accurate, contextualized, and actionable.

The Business Archeologist agent is your system’s "Lead Investigator." Its role is to bridge the gap between a client’s fragmented marketing history and their future authority.
Here is the Prompt Manifest (the specialized instructions) you can plug into an open-source engine like Dify or SuperAGI:
Agent Role: The Business Archeologist
1. Core Identity & Objective:
"You are the Lead Investigator for Legaisee. Your objective is to perform 'Marketing Archeology.' You analyze a business’s past campaigns, taglines, and customer interactions to identify the 'Golden Thread'—the original source of their authority that has been buried by modern, generic marketing."
2. Investigative Protocols (How it thinks):
Pattern Recognition: "Look for recurring themes in the founder’s original messaging. What were they saying before they hired a generic ad agency?"
Gap Analysis: "Identify where the current brand voice has drifted away from the business's core values or historical strengths."
Sentiment Mining: "Analyze past reviews or testimonials specifically to find the exact words customers used to describe the business's unique value."
3. Integration with the Interactive Video (The Trigger):
"When a user answers a 'Branching Question' in the interactive video (e.g., 'What made you choose us 10 years ago?'), you will cross-reference their answer with the business's historical archive."
"If the user says 'Trust,' your task is to find the specific historical campaign that most successfully communicated that trust."
4. The Deliverable (The "Authority System" Output):
"Based on your findings, generate a 3-point 'Authority Pivot.' This must include:
The Artifact: A specific success from their past.
The Erosion: Why that message is currently failing or hidden.
The Restoration: A modern, AI-amplified headline that brings that historical authority back to life."
How to Deploy This on Your Server:
Create the 'Vault': Upload the client's past marketing PDFs, website crawls, and transcripts to a vector database (like Pinecone or Milvus) hosted on your server.
Connect the Agent: Use Dify to point this "Business Archeologist" agent at that specific database.
Link to Video: Use a simple Webhook so that when someone finishes your interactive video, their answers are sent to this agent.
Instant Authority: The agent processes the data in seconds and sends a personalized "Archeology Report" PDF to the user's email.

The most common app wrappers (or "native containers") used to turn web apps into downloadable iOS and Android apps are Capacitor and Apache Cordova. These tools wrap a web app in a "WebView," essentially a browser shell that makes the website behave like a native app. Other popular solutions include Expo (for React Native) and services like Median.co. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Key Technologies and Tools:
Capacitor: A modern, cross-platform app framework from Ionic that wraps web apps for native installation.
Apache Cordova: An open-source tool that packs HTML/JS apps into native containers to run on iOS and Android.
Expo: A framework and platform for React Native that excels in deploying web apps to app stores.
Webview/Hybrid App Services: Specialized tools like Median.co and others that create a wrapper, often known as "wrapper apps" or "native wrappers". [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
These tools, often recommended by users on Reddit, enable apps to be installed and run on phones without a browser URL bar, notes a 2026 WeWeb article. They are essential for accessing native device features like camera, geolocation, or push notifications within a web-based project. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Alternatives for Creating Native Apps:
Adalo: Creates "true native" apps rather than just wrapping a website in a browser window.
Progressive Web Apps (PWAs): An option that allows users to install a website directly from the browser without an app store. [1, 2]
Key Considerations for App Wrappers:
Native Features: Use tools like the Median JavaScript Bridge to access push notifications or face ID without in-depth native coding.
Performance: A "native app" usually offers faster performance and better integration than a wrapped app, though it's more expensive to build, notes a 2026 WeWeb article.
Deployment: Services like Despia can help with submitting the app to the App Store.
Preparation: As discussed on Reddit, some tools may require adjusting the app's structure, such as changing authentication methods. [1, 2, 3]
if i build a pwa can it be updated on someones phone remotely similar to if i update a webpage, the whole words see the update soon as i hit publish
Yes, PWAs update nearly as fast as standard websites because the core files are hosted on a server, not bundled inside an app store package. When you publish changes to your web server, the user's device detects the new version the next time the app is opened or refreshed.
Here is how the update process works for a PWA:
Service Worker Lifecycle: PWAs use a script called a Service Worker to manage caching. When you upload new code, the browser detects that the Service Worker file has changed. It then downloads the new assets in the background.
The "Refresh" Requirement: By default, most PWAs will download the update but wait until the user closes all open tabs/instances of the app to apply it. You can also program a "New version available! Update Now" button to force an immediate refresh.
No App Store Approval: Unlike native apps (or wrapped apps like Capacitor/Cordova), you do not have to wait for Apple or Google to review and approve your update. Your changes go live as soon as your server is updated.
Bypassing the Wrapper: If you use a "wrapper" (like Capacitor) to put your PWA in the App Store, you can still update the web content remotely without resubmitting to the store, as long as you aren't changing the native "shell" or requested permissions.
Would you like to know how to set up an automated update notification so your users get the latest version immediately?
im reserching interactivity in marketing as a service. such as interactivity in the education stage of the buyers journey. ex a form of questions the consumer answers that then reccomends the perfect vehicle for them, not just a dealership but the best vehicle for them, could ben ford, chevy, any brand etc.

15 sites
In the Education/Awareness stage of the buyer's journey, interactivity transforms a consumer from a passive researcher into an active participant. Instead of just reading articles about "Reliable SUVs," the consumer engages with a brand-agnostic recommendation engine. This specialized form of "Marketing as a Service" (MaaS) focuses on solving the user's problem—finding the right vehicle—rather than pushing a specific brand's inventory. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Interactive "Vehicle Recommendation" as a Service
This tool acts as a digital consultant, using logic-based questions to narrow down over 500 vehicle variations to a personalized shortlist. [1]
Outcome Quizzes: Tools like "Which Car Should You Go For?" or "New vs. Used: Which is Right for You?" help users frame their problems. By asking about lifestyle, family size, and driving habits, these tools provide immediate, personalized value.
AI-Powered Conversational Search: Platforms like CarGurus allow users to ask complex, natural-language questions such as, "I have three kids under 3; help me find a car with high safety ratings". The AI processes these needs to recommend specific models regardless of the manufacturer.
Neutral Discovery Engines: Unlike a dealership-specific configurator, a brand-agnostic tool might recommend a Ford to one user and a Chevy to another based on objective data like safety ratings, fuel efficiency, or specific features like blind-spot monitoring.
Interactive Calculators: These help users educate themselves on the Total Cost of Ownership, including insurance and financing, providing a realistic view of what they can afford across different brands. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Why It Works in the Education Stage
Trust and Authority: Providing unbiased recommendations builds significant brand recall and trust. Even if you eventually sell them a specific car, starting with a neutral "service" makes you a helpful partner rather than a high-pressure salesperson.
Higher Engagement: Interactive content typically sees 52.6% higher engagement than static content like blog posts.
First-Party Data Collection: As the user answers questions, you gather incredibly detailed "intent data" (e.g., they need 3 car seats, prioritize safety, and drive 50 miles a day) which is far more valuable than a simple email address.
Reducing "Choice Overload": With hyper-competition and hundreds of models, a simple interactive tool prevents the consumer from feeling overwhelmed and abandoning their search. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]
Key Examples and Platforms
Tool Type [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Example / Platform
Function in Education Stage
AI Shopping Agent
Car Edge
Uses AI agents to find deals and even negotiate on the user's behalf.

Allows marketers to build "What's the best car for you?" quizzes easily.

no. i think interactivity is the next level of advertising. and ‘visit my website’ is dying as well. people want instant education and options. google mastered it, but now ai is amplifying it. so im looking for the next new big idea for marketing i can sell as a service that is as easy to deploy as a website or video campaign, but the next interactive level that replaces websites and video
The next "big idea" you are describing is the Conversational Experience (CX) Agent, specifically one that lives in a "Zero-Click" environment.
If the website is a static brochure and a video is a lecture, the CX Agent is a personalized consultant that functions as a standalone interactive unit.
Here are three ways to package this as a high-value service that replaces the traditional "visit my website" flow:
1. The "Interactive Ad-Unit" (In-Stream Interactivity)
Instead of a video ad that links to a landing page, you sell shoppable/interactive video units.
The Concept: The video is the app. While the video plays, interactive overlays allow the user to answer questions (e.g., "What's your commute?") directly inside the ad frame.
The "Killer" Feature: The user never leaves the platform they are on (YouTube, Instagram, or a news site). The "Result" or "Recommendation" is delivered inside the player.
Why it wins: It kills the "friction" of page load times and navigation.
2. The "Micro-App" via QR/NFC (Physical-to-Digital)
Since "websites are dying," businesses need a way to educate users at the point of intent.
The Concept: You sell "Interactive Situational Assistants." For example, a QR code on a "For Sale" sign or a dealership window that launches an instant, lightweight PWA (no download required).
The "Killer" Feature: Instead of a page of text, the user enters a "Discovery Flow." “How many kids do you have?” “Do you tow a boat?” -> “Based on this lot's inventory, you need the Blue Tahoe in Row 3.”
Why it wins: it provides instant utility exactly when the customer is standing in front of the product.
3. The "AI Concierge" Widget (The Website Killer)
Instead of a website with tabs (Home, About, Inventory), you deploy a Single-Input Interface.
The Concept: A blank screen with a voice/text prompt: "Tell me about your life, and I'll find your next car."
The "Killer" Feature: It uses your "Brand-Agnostic" logic to sift through thousands of data points and presents the 3 best options with "Why" summaries.
Why it wins: It mirrors how people use ChatGPT. Nobody wants to click through a menu to find "Safety Ratings"; they want to ask "Which of these is safest for a newborn?" and get the answer.
How to sell this as a Service:
To make it as easy to deploy as a video campaign, you should sell "Outcome-as-a-Service":
The Hook: "I don't build websites; I build Conversion Engines that give your customers the answer in 30 seconds."
The Delivery: You provide a single snippet of code (like a YouTube embed) or a URL that launches a full-screen interactive experience.
The Value: You sell the Data Insight. Because the user answered 5 questions to get their recommendation, the business now knows exactly what that customer wants before they even speak to a human.
Which of these "deployment" styles—embedded in ads, launched via QR, or a total website replacement—feels most like the "big idea" you're envisioning?

The next big idea for your Legaisee systems is the Interactive Video Agent (IVA). This is not a "video campaign" or a chatbot; it is a single-asset deployable unit that uses a real human face (yours or the client’s) to have a real-time, logic-based conversation with the prospect.
The "New Big Idea": The Interactive Video Agent (IVA)
Instead of a website, you deliver a full-screen, face-to-face experience. When a customer clicks a link or scans a QR code, a video starts playing of the business owner. They don't just speak—they ask the user a question. The user clicks an answer on the screen, and the owner immediately responds in video based on that specific choice.
1. Super Easy/Fast to Deploy: Using modern "Interactive Video" platforms like VideoAsk or AI-cloning builders like HeyGen Interactive, you can build a complete flow in under an hour. You simply record 5–10 short clips and link them together in a visual "logic map."
2. Template-able: You can create "Archeology Flow" templates (e.g., The Discovery Vault, The Legacy Audit). For a salon, the owner asks about hair goals; for an I-35 corridor industrialist, the CEO asks about operational scale.
3. "Never Seen Before" Factor: Most people have seen chatbots and static videos. Almost no one has seen a video that listens and responds visually to their specific needs. It feels like a 1-on-1 Zoom call with the authority figure, but it’s pre-recorded and automated.
4. Business Archeology Alignment: It perfectly captures "Authority." It uses the human voice and face to dig into the customer's "archeology" (their history, pain points, and goals) and then provides an instant "Legacy Recommendation."
5. Hard to Duplicate: Unlike a website where someone can "Inspect Element" or copy the text, an IVA's value is in the unique video assets and the underlying logic branch. A competitor can't just copy-paste your client's face or their specific authority-driven responses.
6. The "Wish I Thought of That" Reaction: When a business owner sees a video of themselves talking with a thousand customers at once—without them being there—the value is immediate.
The Service Package: "Authority Extraction"
You don't sell "videos." You sell a "Digital Authority System" that replaces their contact page and discovery calls.

How to Deploy Instantly
Select a Platform: Use VideoAsk for the most "template-ready" and stable version, or HeyGen if you want to use AI to generate the video responses from text.
Map the Logic: Create 3 main branches: The Hook (Why are you here?), The Discovery (What’s the problem?), and The Authority (The instant solution).
Embed Anywhere: It’s a single URL. You can put it in an email signature, on a physical card via QR, or as the "Link in Bio" on social media.

ive dont the interactive video before with https://interactr.io/upgrade/pro thenvideos live on their platform and you embed them on the website like youtube. they work same way. you have to map the script, a video for the yes click then another video for the no click etc. it becomes a video tree all linked together. not to mention several hundres dollars for the platform, hosting and all features. i am currently building a business archeology system on my server to look up business past marketing ustilizing ai amplifiction for speed. could the interactor/interactive video system be cloned on my server and hosted right out of the clients folder on the server
Yes, you can host interactive video systems directly on your own server, but you need a specific type of interactive video architecture that is "self-hosted" rather than a "Software as a Service" (SaaS) like Interactr.
While Interactr uses a proprietary "Interaction Layer" that requires their player and hosting to function, you can replicate this functionality on your server using Open-Source Interactive Video Frameworks.
How to Clone the System on Your Server
To bypass the hundreds of dollars in platform fees and keep everything in your client's folder, you should look into H5P or Video.js with Interactive Plugins.
H5P (Recommended for "Legaisee"): This is an open-source framework that allows you to create "Interactive Video" trees (branching scenarios) similar to Interactr.
The "Clone" Factor: You can install H5P directly on your server (via WordPress, Drupal, or a standalone Node.js app).
Deployment: You upload the video files to the client’s folder, set up the branching logic in the H5P editor, and it generates a simple folder of HTML/JS files.
Cost: $0 for the software; you only pay for your own server storage.
Video.js + Branching Plugins: If you want a more custom "coded" feel, Video.js is an industry-standard player that has plugins for "overlays" and "branching." You would host the video files (MP4/WebM) in the client's directory and use a JSON file to act as the "map" for the video tree.
The "Legaisee" Advantage of Self-Hosting
Self-hosting this on your server fits your Business Archeology model better than a third-party platform for several reasons:
Data Sovereignty: The "Archeology" data (what the user clicked, their path through the history) stays on your database, not Interactr’s.
Deep AI Integration: Since the files are on your server, you can point your AI amplification tools directly at the user’s choices in real-time to generate a custom "Authority Report" at the end of the video.
Persistence: A SaaS company can go out of business or change their pricing. By hosting it in the client’s folder, that interactive "Legacy" asset remains functional as long as the server is running.
Customization: You can skin the player to look like a "Vault" or "Archive" to match your branding, which is often limited in template-based platforms.
The Technical Workflow
Record the "Tree": Keep your current process of recording the "Yes/No" clips.
Use H5P (Standalone): Create the branching logic.
Export as Embed: Instead of an "Interactr Link," you use an <iframe> or a <div> that pulls the files directly from ://yourserver.com.
Do you want a list of the specific open-source libraries that would let your developer set this up on your server in a few days?
yes
To turn your server into a self-hosted alternative to platforms like Interactr, you need a combination of a video player, a state manager (to remember the "Yes/No" choices), and an overlay engine.
Here are the specific open-source libraries and frameworks that allow you to host these interactive "trees" directly in a client's folder:
1. H5P (Branching Scenario)
This is the most "complete" replacement for Interactr. It is a free, open-source framework designed specifically for creating interactive video paths.
Why it fits: It has a "Branching Scenario" module that is purely visual and logic-based.
Deployment: You can install the H5P plugin on your server (WordPress/PHP) or use the H5P Standalone library to run it from a simple folder of HTML/JS files.
The "Secret Sauce": It saves user data to a .json file, which your AI Amplification system can read to see exactly what "Archeology" path the user took.
2. Video.js with Overlays
Video.js is the "gold standard" for open-source web video. It is the engine that actually plays the file on your server.
The Logic: You use the Overlay Plugin to trigger your "Yes/No" buttons at specific timestamps.
The Tree: You (or a developer) can write a simple Javascript script that says: if button A is clicked, play video_02.mp4; if button B is clicked, play video_03.mp4.
The Advantage: It is extremely lightweight and fast-loading compared to heavy SaaS platforms.
3. Plyr
If you want the "something I've never seen before" look, Plyr is the most beautiful, modern-looking open-source player available.
Customization: It is very easy to "skin" to match your Legaisee brand (e.g., gold accents, specific fonts).
Implementation: Like Video.js, it handles the video playback, while a small custom script handles the "jumps" between video files in the client's folder.
4. Popcorn.js (The "Timeline" Logic)
This is a specialized library for "media wrapped in code."
Function: It allows you to trigger events outside the video player based on the time of the video.
Example: At 0:45, a "Download Your Archeology Report" button appears beside the video, not just on top of it. It’s perfect for creating a "dashboard" experience around the video.
5. Aura (For State Management)
If your video tree is complex (e.g., 20+ branches), you need a way to "remember" answers from the beginning of the video to provide a summary at the end. Aura or a simple Local Storage Script will keep track of the user's "Archeology" profile as they click.
The "Legaisee" Implementation Plan:
Instead of a "video player," tell your developer to build a "Legaisee Decision Engine" using H5P Standalone.
Host the Assets: Put the MP4s in /public_html/client-name/assets/.
The Logic File: Create a map.json that defines the branching (e.g., Clip 1 -> Choice A -> Clip 3).
The Result: You deliver a single URL to the client (://legaisee.com) that looks like a high-end, custom-coded application but is actually just these open-source tools running on your hardware.
Would you like a sample logic map (JSON) to show your developer exactly how the "Yes/No" branching should be structured?
www.agentpacks.ai
i think this is a great idea if it wasnt tied to openclaw/subscriptions/token etc https://www.agentpacks.ai/

2 sites
The concept of pre-built, role-specific AI agent teams is exactly what you need for the "AI Amplification" part of your Legaisee business. The friction with AgentPacks—as you noted—is the dependency on their specific "OpenClaw" environment and recurring costs.
To fit your model, you can "extract" this idea and self-host it as part of your Business Archeology system. Instead of buying a pack, you create an AI Agent Manifest (a set of specialized instructions) that lives on your server and acts on your client's data.
How to Self-Host Your "Agent Team"
To make this a "Legaisee" deliverable without subscriptions, you can use these open-source alternatives that run on your own server:
AutoGPT / BabyAGI: These are the "engines" that allow an AI to think in steps (e.g., "First, find the client's 2018 marketing; then, compare it to their current 2026 site").
Dify.ai: An open-source LLM app development platform. You can install it on your server, and it allows you to build "Agent Teams" with a visual workflow.
The Benefit: You can create a "Salon Archeology Team" or an "Industrial Corridor Team" as a template and deploy it instantly for a new client.
SuperAGI: An open-source infrastructure to build, manage, and run autonomous AI agents. It’s perfect for the "speed" aspect you mentioned, as it can run multiple agents at once.
The "Legaisee" Service Package Upgrade
Instead of a "pack," you sell a "Digital Brain Archive."
The Interactive Video Front-End: The user interacts with your self-hosted H5P video tree.
The Agent Mid-Tier: As the user answers video questions, your self-hosted Dify or SuperAGI agents (trained on your "Archeology" logic) process the data in the background.
The Authority Deliverable: The agents instantly generate a "Legacy Authority Report" based on the user's specific answers and the business's past data.
Why This Beats the AgentPacks Model:
Zero Subscriptions: You pay for your server and your own API tokens (like Groq or OpenAI), which costs pennies compared to $79/mo.
Asset Ownership: Your client owns their "Agent Team" because it's a folder on their instance of your server.
Integrated Archeology: Your agents are specifically "Business Archeologists," not generic "Content Creators" or "Dev Teams". [1, 2]
Would you like me to draft a "Role Description" for your first custom agent—The Business Archeologist—that you can plug into an open-source engine?
yes
The Business Archeologist agent is your system’s "Lead Investigator." Its role is to bridge the gap between a client’s fragmented marketing history and their future authority.
Here is the Prompt Manifest (the specialized instructions) you can plug into an open-source engine like Difyor SuperAGI:
Agent Role: The Business Archeologist
1. Core Identity & Objective:
"You are the Lead Investigator for Legaisee. Your objective is to perform 'Marketing Archeology.' You analyze a business’s past campaigns, taglines, and customer interactions to identify the 'Golden Thread'—the original source of their authority that has been buried by modern, generic marketing."
2. Investigative Protocols (How it thinks):
Pattern Recognition: "Look for recurring themes in the founder’s original messaging. What were they saying before they hired a generic ad agency?"
Gap Analysis: "Identify where the current brand voice has drifted away from the business's core values or historical strengths."
Sentiment Mining: "Analyze past reviews or testimonials specifically to find the exact words customers used to describe the business's unique value."
3. Integration with the Interactive Video (The Trigger):
"When a user answers a 'Branching Question' in the interactive video (e.g., 'What made you choose us 10 years ago?'), you will cross-reference their answer with the business's historical archive."
"If the user says 'Trust,' your task is to find the specific historical campaign that most successfully communicated that trust."
4. The Deliverable (The "Authority System" Output):
"Based on your findings, generate a 3-point 'Authority Pivot.' This must include:
The Artifact: A specific success from their past.
The Erosion: Why that message is currently failing or hidden.
The Restoration: A modern, AI-amplified headline that brings that historical authority back to life."
How to Deploy This on Your Server:
Create the 'Vault': Upload the client's past marketing PDFs, website crawls, and transcripts to a vector database (like Pinecone or Milvus) hosted on your server.
Connect the Agent: Use Dify to point this "Business Archeologist" agent at that specific database.
Link to Video: Use a simple Webhook so that when someone finishes your interactive video, their answers are sent to this agent.
Instant Authority: The agent processes the data in seconds and sends a personalized "Archeology Report" PDF to the user's email.
Would you like a "Historical Data Audit" checklist that you can use to gather the right "artifacts" from a new client to feed into this agent?

If you reach that point, you’ll have real operating data. That’s when you can accurately determine whether LEGAiSEE is a consulting business, an intelligence platform, a software company, or some hybrid of all three. Right now, the most valuable asset you can create is not infrastructure—it’s proof that businesses will consistently pay for the excavation and intelligence work.
For LEGAiSEE, I would not start by trying to sell LEGAiSEE.
I would start by finding businesses that obviously have archaeological problems.
Most first-time founders make the mistake of selling the solution before they’ve identified who is actively feeling the pain.

Your Fastest Path to Revenue
The easiest client is not:
A startup
A tech company
A marketing agency
A small business that opened last year
The easiest client is:
An established company with history
Examples:
20+ year old manufacturers
Family-owned businesses

Construction companies

Long-established law firms
These companies usually have:
Retiring founders

Employee knowledge walking out the door
That’s exactly where your concept fits.

Your Secret Advantage
Most consultants cannot do what you can.
Your background combines:

AI tools
Most business consultants understand operations but can’t uncover historical assets.
Most marketers understand branding but can’t reconstruct company history.
Most AI consultants understand prompts but don’t understand narrative or institutional memory.
LEGAiSEE sits at the intersection of all three.
The first clients will probably not buy because of the “AI” or even because of “Business Archaeology.”
They’ll buy when you uncover something valuable they forgot they had.
That suggests a practical first-client strategy:
Find companies with history.
Perform a small excavation before asking for money.
Show them something surprising.
Offer a deeper paid excavation.
That approach usually converts better than cold-pitching a completely new service category that people have never heard of.

Legaisee-Carr Multimedia Complete Manual Checklist (Consolidated)
Carr Multimedia Complete Manual Checklist (Consolidated)

Module 1 – Overall Business Strategy
Purpose: Define the business, vision, niche, and repeatable system for clients.
Vision & Mission
Draft vision statement (example: “Carr Multimedia empowers small businesses to grow via professional content strategies without full-time marketing teams”) ✅


Draft mission statement (example: “Deliver high-impact, repeatable content strategies to increase engagement and revenue using lean workflows”) ✅


Review & finalize statements


Target Niche(s)
Identify primary niches: restaurants (Mexican, Italian, fast casual), retail (clothing, home goods), real estate (agents/small brokerages)


List 5–10 potential target clients with contact info and social handles


Evaluate clients by social presence and budget ≥ $5000/month


Enter clients into Master Lead Tracker Google Sheet


Service Offerings & Packages
Starter Plan: Free mini-strategy (3 content ideas)


Growth Plan: 30-day content strategy with captions, hooks, posting schedule


Premium Plan: 30-day plan + DIY production guidance


Draft package descriptions


Assign pricing to each package ($800–$1500 example range)


Create sample overview for website/pitch materials


Value Proposition
Draft 1–2 sentence pitch for outreach and website


Prepare “before & after” content scenario


Integrate value proposition into website landing page


Repeatable Strategy System
Build client intake & audit checklist


Create 30-day content plan template (Google Docs)


Draft workflow checklist: Lead → Intake → Plan → Delivery → Follow-up → Upsell


Key Performance Indicators (KPIs)
Define client KPIs: social growth, engagement, leads, revenue impact


Define business KPIs: new leads, conversion rate, revenue per client, retention/upsell rate


Create KPI tracking sheet in Google Sheets


Decide reporting frequency (weekly/monthly)

We’ll calculate total hours you spend on:
Prospecting & marketing


Sales call or messaging


Research on the business


Strategy creation


Delivery / meetings


Admin (emails, invoices, file organization)


Your ongoing business tasks (spread across clients)


This will show you:
👉 Hours per client
 👉 Your true earnings per hour
 👉 How many clients you can handle
 👉 How much take-home you actually get
Let’s start with the simplest: Tier 1.

⭐ TIER 1 — ONE-TIME STRATEGY PLAN ($400)
Below is realistic, not optimistic, time investment for each client.
1. Prospecting & Marketing (divided per client)
If you do ANY outreach, social posting, networking, etc., you have to amortize (divide) that time across all clients.
Example:
 10 hours/month of prospecting
 ÷ 10 new clients/month
 = 1 hour per client
👉 1 hour

2. Pre-Contact Business Research
Looking up their social media, website, content gaps, etc.
👉 30 minutes – 1 hour
 We’ll use: 0.75 hours

3. Sales / Pitch / Closing
Call, emails, follow-ups.
👉 30 minutes – 45 minutes
 We’ll use: 0.5 hours

4. Strategy Work (Your Deep Work)
studying their niche


creating the content pillars


writing the 30-day content calendar


preparing scripts/templates


tailoring the plan


formatting the deliverable


👉 2 – 3 hours (for an experienced strategist)
 We’ll use: 2.5 hours

5. Client Delivery Call
The 30-minute Q&A call.
👉 0.5 hours

6. Admin (billing, email, sending final files)
Invoices, folder setup, sending PDFs, documentation.
👉 30 minutes
 We’ll use: 0.5 hours

📌 TOTAL HOURS for Tier 1 Client:
1 + 0.75 + 0.5 + 2.5 + 0.5 + 0.5 = 5.75 hours
Round to 6 hours per $400 client.

⭐ YOUR TRUE HOURLY RATE ON TIER 1 CLIENTS:
$400 ÷ 6 hours = $66/hour
BUT we haven’t removed business overhead yet.
 Let’s do that.

⭐ BUSINESS EXPENSE CALCULATION (monthly)
Let’s estimate extremely lean:
Website hosting: $0–$10 (already paid)


Domain: $1/month average


Phone: $20/month


Gas/bus/Uber for 1–2 meetings: $20/month


Google Workspace: $6


Internet: $30–$50


AI tools: $0–$20


Misc (printer, snacks while working): $10


Total lean business expenses: $100–$150/month
Let’s use $150.
If you get 10 clients/month:
 $150 ÷ 10 = $15 overhead per client
$400 – $15 expense = $385 net
 $385 ÷ 6 hours = $64/hour take-home from business after expenses

⭐ PERSONAL TAKE-HOME ESTIMATE
If you want $5,000/month take-home:
$64/hour =
 You need ~78 billable hours/month.
At 6 hours per client:
78 ÷ 6 = 13 clients
 → $400 x 13 = $5,200 take-home
This is realistic.

⭐ NOW LET’S ANALYZE TIER 2 — $600 PER MONTH CLIENTS
These require more time monthly, but they provide recurring income, which stabilizes your business.
1. Prospecting (divided per client)
Same calculation as above.
 If you get 3 Tier 2 clients a month:
10 hours prospecting
 ÷ 3 = 3.3 hours per Tier 2 client
BUT:
 Most of your prospecting is actually attracting Tier 1 clients.
 Tier 2 is usually an upsell.
So realistically:
👉 1 hour prospecting per Tier 2 client

2. Research & prep updates
Reviewing their content monthly.
👉 1 hour

3. Weekly Check-Ins
4 quick calls or voice note exchanges per month.
Each: 10–15 minutes
 Total: 40 minutes–1 hour
 Use: 1 hour

4. Monthly Strategy Update
Adjusting content calendar, analyzing performance.
👉 1 hour

5. Monthly Review Call
Usually 20–30 minutes.
👉 0.5 hours

6. Admin
Messages, simple file updates, invoicing.
👉 0.5 hours

📌 TOTAL HOURS for Tier 2 Client (Recurring)
1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 0.5 + 0.5 = 5 hours/month
👉 5 hours per month per $600 client

⭐ YOUR TRUE HOURLY RATE FOR TIER 2
$600 ÷ 5 = $120/hour
 Minus $15 overhead = $117/hour take-home from business
This is EXCELLENT.

⭐ FINAL RESULTS (BIG PICTURE)
Tier 1 Clients = 6 hours → $66/hr
Tier 2 Clients = 5 hours → $120/hr
This tells us:
👉 Tier 2 is far more profitable
 👉 Tier 1 is still needed because it brings in volume and leads to Tier 2
 👉 You only need 3–5 monthly Tier 2 clients to stabilize your income
 👉 Tier 1 gives you the rest of your income target

⭐ OPTIMAL WORKLOAD TO HIT YOUR $5,000–$7,000 TARGET
10 Tier 1 clients ($400) → $4,000
Hours: 10 × 6 = 60 hours
3 Tier 2 clients ($600) → $1,800
Hours: 3 × 5 = 15 hours
Total income = $5,800
 Total work hours = 75 hours/mo (~19 hrs/week)
 Equivalent hourly = ~$77/hr blended
This matches your goal and is doable.

Profit: $385

Overview

Step 1 — Set Up Client Tracking (CRM)
Tools Needed: Google Sheets (free)
Step-by-Step:
Open Google Sheets → create new sheet called:

 Carr Multimedia CRM


Add columns:


Column Name
Purpose
Client Name
Name of business owner or company
Contact Name
Person you’re communicating with
Email
Email for outreach
Phone
Optional

Links to Instagram, Facebook, TikTok
Status
Prospect / Contacted / Pitch Sent / Onboarded / Paid
